# 제5장

# 결론 및 후속연구 방향

## 5.1 연구 요약 및 결론

본 연구는 유네스코 도시 물관리 우수인증 시범사업을 성공적으로 추진하기 위해 해외의 유사 인증사례를 조사하여 벤치마킹하고, 시범사업 운영과정에서 도출된 문제점에 대한 개선방안을 마련함으로써 세계 인증도시로의 확대를 위한 발판을 마련하기 위해 수행되었다. 또한, 타 물관리 인증제도 등과 연계하여 사업을 브랜딩하기 위한 전략과 향후 본사업이 안정적으로 추진될 수 있도록 인증 업무를 전담할 조직의 설립방안을 다각적인 각도에서 검토하였으며, 개도국 협력사업 발굴 등 향후 인증제도 발전방안을 마련하고자 하였다.

우선, 인증 도시들 간 우수 사례 및 전문성 공유를 위해서는 국제 정보교환 플랫폼이 필요하므로 유네스코 세계유산사업, 유네스코 창의도시 네트워크, 유네스코 글로벌 학습도시 네트워크의 특성과 운영체계를 참고하여 ‘유네스코 도시 물관리 글로벌 네트워크’를 물관리 인증제도의 운영체계로서 제안하였다. 네트워크 운영을 통해 깨끗한 물관리 정책에 대한 공유가 이뤄지고 상호 간의 협력을 증진시키기 위해 전 세계 여러 도시의 물관리를 지원하는 역할을 자발적으로 수행할 수 있을 것으로 기대된다. 네트워크 가입 절차는 멘토 도시는 자동 가입으로, 멘티도시는 신청도시에서 가입신청서 작성 후 해당국 담당기관(IHP 국가위원회)에 제출하여 담당기관의 검토를 거쳐 후보 도시로 선정되고 이후 국제인증센터에서 가입신청서를 평가하여 최종 네트워크 가입이 승인되도록 하면 될 것이다. 물론 네트워크에 가입 시 여러 가지 혜택이 있어야 여러 도시에서 가입을 희망할 것이므로 이에 대한 검토도 이뤄져야 한다. 첫 번째로 유네스코 브랜드를 활용한 도시 이미지 제고 및 홍보의 극대화가 가능할 것이며, 둘째로 멘토(선진국 가입도시)의 경우 멘티(개도국 가입도시) 지원을 통해 물관리 분야 우수 인증 및 물안보에 기여할 수 있는 계기를 제공할 수 있을 것이다. 또한, 네트워크 관련 회의/행사 개최를 통해 지역경제 활성화에 기여할 수 있고 네트워크 도시 간 교류 및 협력을 통해 도시 물관리 시스템의 질적 향상에 기여할 수 있다는 것이 메리트라 할 수 있겠다.

타 우수인증(Seal of Excellence) 사례를 통해서는 우수인증의 궁극적인 목적과 그 효과에 대해서 알아볼 수 있었으며, 도시 물관리 우수인증도 다른 사례들과 마찬가지로 인증을 통해 달성하고자 하는 목표를 명확히 설정하여 추진한다면 전 세계 도시의 먹는물 품질향상과 시민들의 수돗물에 대한 신뢰도 향상에 따른 음용률 제고에 기여할 수 있을 것이다.

2026년부터는 전 세계 ‘도시 물관리 우수인증’ 사업이 공식 출범하여 본격적으로 추진된다. 따라서 물관리 인증제도를 지속적으로 관리 및 운영할 전담 조직 구성이 필수적이며, 이에 가칭 ‘유네스코 도시 물관리 국제인증센터’를 설립하는 방안을 검토하였다. 국제인증센터의 설립 방향은 크게 두 가지로 분류할 수 있으며 하나는 현재의 유네스코 물과학국(Division of Water Sciences) 내 유네스코-IHP 사무국 내부 조직을 통해 유네스코가 직접 운영하는 방안이고 나머지는 유네스코 전문기관(카테고리1 기관/센터 또는 카테고리2 기관/센터)을 설립 또는 연계해 운영하는 방안이다. 어떤 방법을 선택하느냐에 따라 국제인증센터 설립 추진에 소요되는 기간과 행정력 및 효율성이 달라지므로 사업 진행 단계별 방법을 달리하여 유연하게 운영하는 것이 필요하다고 판단된다.

우선 사업 초기(시범사업 단계로 최초 인증도시 발생 시)에는 유네스코-IHP 사무국에서 사업관리와 인증평가 두 가지를 병행 수행하고 사업 정착기(인증이 확대되고 멘토-멘티 지식교류 시작 시)에는 사업 초기와 마찬가지로 유네스코-IHP 사무국에서 제도운영, 일반사무 및 인증평가를 담당하고 지식교류는 유네스코 i-WSSM 등 기존 카테고리 기관에서 담당하는 것이 보다 효율적일 것이다. 사업 안정화 및 확산 단계에서는 제도운영 및 일반사무만 유네스코-IHP 사무국에서 담당하고 인증평가 및 지식교류 부분은 유네스코 도시 물관리 국제인증센터(카테고리 2기관으로 신설)에서 담당하여 수행하고 WHO, UN Water Family 등과 협력하는 것으로 정하는 것이 적합하다고 판단된다.

본 인증사업의 후속사업이나 연계사업으로 가능성이 있는 사업으로는 “기초 위생 인프라 구축”사업이 있는데 이 사업은 깨끗한 물 공급을 위한 상·하수도 개선 및 세수 시설 설치 등 기초 위생 인프라 구축 지원, 공중보건수칙 등 감염병 대응 인식 제고를 위한 것으로서 개도국 수자원 관리를 위한 ODA 사업 지속 추진, WASH 등 식수·위생 관리 종합 사업 확대 등의 항목이 포함되어 있다. 또한 “개도국 사회인프라 지원” 사업과 “인프라 지원 협업체계 강화” 사업 등도 후속 및 연계사업으로 가능할 것으로 보인다.

그리고 후속사업 추진을 위한 재원조달 방안으로는 우리나라 ODA의 무상원조와 유상원조를 활용하는 방법이 있는데, 향후 앞서 언급한 ‘도시 물관리 글로벌 네트워크’가 구축된다면 네트워크를 활용한 ODA 사업발굴이 매우 유리해진다. 멘티 도시로 가입되어 있는 국가에서 사업을 희망하는 경우, 운영위원회와 K-water에서 필요시 현지조사와 사업 타당성을 검토한 후 한국 정부와 협의를 거쳐 협력대상국과 연결시킴으로써 사업발굴과 준비에 소요되는 과정을 상당히 축소할 수 있을 것으로 기대된다. 이후에는 국내 ODA 사업 추진절차대로 시행 계획 심사, 예산심의, 예산·종합시행계획 확정 단계 등을 거쳐 사업에 착수하면 큰 무리 없이 후속사업을 진행할 수 있을 것으로 생각된다.

[그림5-1] 유네스코 도시 물관리 우수인증 발전전략 체계도

![image](image_039.png)

## 5.2 후속연구 방향

도시 물관리 우수인증 시범사업이 UNESCO-IHP 승인 사업에서 UNESCO 총회 승인 사업으로 승격됨에 따라 인증 수여 시기가 기존 ‘24년에서 ’26년으로 순연되어 기존에 예상했던 사업추진 일정보다 수개월이 늦어지게 되었다. 이에 본 연구가 종료되는 시점인 ‘22년 12월 이후에도 시범사업을 원활하게 추진해나가기 위한 후속연구의 수행이 필요하게 되었으며, 후속연구에서는 시범사업이 착수되어 본격적으로 진행되는 시점이 포함되므로 시범사업 참여도시가 우수인증을 수여 받아 향후 본 사업 추진시 모범사례로 활용되고 ’유네스코 도시 물관리 글로벌 네트워크‘에서 멘토로 활동할 수 있도록 지원하기 위한 연구를 중점으로 두어야 할 것이다.

현재 WHO의 인증평가툴 자문안에 대한 합리적이고 객관적인 수용을 통해 인증 평가툴 개정을 진행하고 있는 상태이다. 따라서 인증평가툴의 개정 방향이 어떻게 설정되느냐에 따라 향후 WHO와의 협업 중요도가 매우 높아질 수 있으므로 WHO와의 지속적인 관계 유지와 상호 간의 의견 교류가 활발히 이루어질 수 있게 연구 방향을 설정해야 할 것이다. 또한, 도시 물관리 우수인증 사업의 중장기적 발전을 위해서는 우수인증 전담조직의 구성이 필수적이므로 본 연구를 통해 도출된 “도시 물관리 우수인증 국제인증센터(가칭)” 설립방안을 토대로 가장 적합한 방법에 대한 구상과 이에 대한 타당성을 조사하는 것이 필요하다고 판단된다.

# Seal of Excellence for Urban Water Management

# Guideline for assessment procedure

1. Purpose

The project will seek to support Member States in achieving their SDG 6.1 and SDG 14 target on universal and equitable access to safe and affordable drinking water for all. The project will thus not only promote the provision of safe tap water and increase the tap water consumption but will also advance responsible water resources consumption, increase public awareness and eventually enhance the management of the water system. The project, by reducing the number of plastic bottles used and discarded per year, will contribute to the mitigation of climate change and minimization of waste being landfilled or finding its way to the water bodies and aquatic life.

Furthermore, the sealed city will have to identify another city in a developing country to twin and support technically, in order for the latter to improve its capacity and pursue the award process. This action will promote international cooperation and technology transfer, contributing to Member States efforts in achieving SDG target 17.6.

The purpose of this guideline is to present the procedure for the assessment of the urban water supply system operation and management (qualitative assessment) and drinking water quality (quantitative assessment) of an applicant city in a developed country for the UNESCO Seal of Excellence for Urban Water Management.

2. Scope of Project

2.1 This guideline is prepared mainly based on the Guidelines for Drinking-water Quality by the World Health Organization (WHO) and Water Safety Plan (WSP) resources by WHO and the International Water Association (IWA), which may be applied to the applicant cities.

2.2 The first stage is to assess the water quality for drinking water of water treatment plant, water supply area and tap in an applicant city.

2.3 The second stage is to assess the urban water supply system operation and management of an applicant city including water resources, water treatment process and the water supply network.

3. Roles and Responsibilities

4. Procedures for the Seal

4.1 Seal Criteria

An applicant city is sealed by drinking water quality assessment (30%), and water supply system operation and management assessment (70%), which are further describes in chapter 5 (Assessment of Drinking Water Quality), and chapter 6 (Assessment of Water Supply System Operation and Management).

4.2 Seal Process

## 4.2.1 <u>Step 1. Applying</u> for the <u>Seal</u>

(1) Applicant city prepares the application form and submits it to UNESCO. [Annex 2]

(2) UNESCO reviews the application form and approves or not to proceed it.

## 4.2.2 <u>Step 2. Organizing the Seal assessment units</u>

(1) UNESCO requests additional documents of related to the water supply system information and application fee to applicant city. [Annex 3, 10]

(2) Applicant city make additional documents and submits it to UNESCO with the application fee.

(3) UNESCO organizes the Seal assessment units in order to proceed qualitative and quantitative assessment.

## 4.2.3 <u>Step 3. Establishing</u> the <u>details</u> of <u>Seal assessment</u>

(1) For the qualitative assessment, UNESCO requests both assessment team and technical advisor to review documents and additional information in advance. And UNESCO also builds the detailed field survey schedule in consultation with assessment team and applicant city.

(2) For quantitative assessment, UNESCO requests both technical advisor and applicant city to set up water quality parameters and choose an ISO certified laboratory for water quality analysis (quantitative assessment). And then UNESCO confirms water quality parameters and who, when, where, and how to sample water with technical advisor, applicant city, and the ISO certified laboratory. [Annex 5, 5-1, 5-2]

## 4.2.4 <u>Step 4. Doing the qualitative and quantitative assessment</u>

# 4.2.4.1 Qualitative Assessment

(1) Prior to the field survey, assessment team shall understand the Seal assessment tool-kit usage. [Annex 10]

(2) Assessment team assesses the performance level of water supply system operation and management using tool-kit with the help of technical advisor. [Annex 4-1, 4-2, 4-3]

(3) Qualitative Assessment shall be conducted at once during a year.

(4) When the qualitative assessment is finished, assessment team submits the results to technical advisor.

4.2.4.2 Quantitative Assessment

(1) UNESCO (or technical advisor) request for an ISO certified laboratory to begin water quality analysis. An ISO certified laboratory shall report the results to technical advisor immediately after finishing water quality analysis. [Annex 4]

(2) Technical advisor, and/or national surveillance agency supervise water sampling if necessary.

(3) Quantitative assessment shall be conducted quarterly during a year.

## 4.2.5 <u>Step 5. Verifying and reporting the assessment results</u>

(1) ISO certified laboratory and technical advisor verify the quantitative assessment results. And technical advisor submits the final report of quantitative assessment and results to the UNESCO. [Annex 6]

(2) Assessment team and technical advisor verify the qualitative assessment results. And technical advisor submits the final report of qualitative assessment and results to the UNESCO. [Annex 7]

(1) UNESCO reviews the final assessment reports and submits them to The Board of Seal of Excellence.

(2) The Board of Seal of Excellence reviews the final assessment reports and makes the final decision. If applicant city doesn’t agree with the final decision, it can ask for coordination/clarification. [Annex 8]

## 4.2.6 <u>Step 6. Awarding the Seal of Excellence</u>

(1) UNESCO awards the Seal of Excellence to the applicant city depending on the final decision of Board of Seal of Excellence.

(1) Sealed city pays the membership fee annually and joins a knowledge-sharing platform which is coordinated by UNESCO.

(2) UNESCO shall inform the successful achievement of sealed city to UNESCO members. No information related to the application will be shared nor unsuccessful assessment.

4.3 Award system

Award is decided by the sum of both the qualitative and quantitative assessment results. The assessment of the water supply system operation and management will comprise 70% of overall assessment results. The assessment of drinking water quality analysis will comprise 30%. When the sum of both results is more than 90%, it is a success for the seal. Less than 85% is a fail for the seal. If the sum of both results is between 85% and 90%, it is classified as a conditional success and given the opportunity to assess again within a year.

| Rating | Success | Conditional Success | Fail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Total Score | ≥ 90% | 90%> x ≥ 85% | < 85% |

\* Seen in 5.5.7 and 6.7.3 for more detail on calculation of score

4.4 Application and Membership Fee

The applicant city for this seal shall pay the application fee and assessment fee. Assessment fee is actual costs that include airfare, staying cost, labour fee, and training expense for Assessment Team members. The Sealed city shall pay the membership fee annually as follows.

<table>
<tr><th>Population</th><th>Application fee</th><th>Assessment fee</th><th>Membership fee (Annual)</th></tr>
<tr><td>Below 100,000</td><td rowspan="4">2,000 USD</td><td rowspan="4">*Actual costs for field survey and analysis, etc.</td><td>3,000 USD</td></tr>
<tr><td>100,000 ~ 300,000</td><td>10,000 USD</td></tr>
<tr><td>300,000 ~ 500,000</td><td>20,000 USD</td></tr>
<tr><td>Above 500,000</td><td>30,000 USD</td></tr>
</table>

4.5 Validity period of Seal of Excellence

Validity period is four year. After the first seal, audit (re-assessment) for the Seal validity is performed at three year as shown in the guideline.

### 5 Assessment of Drinking Water Quality (Quantitative Assessment)

5.1 Overview

5.1.1 Coverage

This guideline is for the assessment of drinking water quality in applicant city.

5.1.2 Frequency of assessment

Assessment of drinking water is performed quarterly in a year.

5.2 Documents and Records Management

5.2.1 Final confirmed assessment report of drinking water is submitted to the Board of Seal of Excellence.

5.2.2 Assessment report of drinking water quality shall be preserved for at least three years with at least one copy by UNESCO, applicant city, and technical advisor.

5.3 Preparation of Assessment

5.3.1 UNESCO shall send document form of drinking water analysis information to applicant city just after approval of UNESCO. Applicant city creates the document and submits to UNESCO. (Annex 3)

5.3.2 If all documents are all right, UNESCO will select an ISO certified laboratory. The applicant city shall request tests of the drinking water quality from the UNESCO designated ISO certified laboratory.

5.3.3 UNESCO selects sampling observation organization.

5.4 Assessment Scope

5.4.1 Quantitative assessment scope is a water supply system from water treatment plant (treated water) to water supply area and tap (supplied water) in applicant city.

5.4.2 When selecting sampling locations for assessment of drinking water quality, applicant city shall discuss with technical advisor by considering water supply system.

5.5 Assessment Procedures

5.5.1 Determination of the number of sampling for analysis

Sampling for analysis shall be the water treatment plants, water supply area and taps in applicant city.

(1) The number of sampling for water treatment plants shall be all in operation.

(2) The number of sampling in water supply area and taps shall be decided by water

supply population of applicant city as follows in the table.

| Population | The number of sampling |
| --- | --- |
| <5,000 | 10 |
| 5,000 \~ 100,000 | 25 |
| >100,000 \~ 500,000 | 50 |
| >500,000 | Max 100 |

5.2.2 Selection of sampling locations

Regarding sampling points for analysis including the water treatment plants, water supply area and tap, it is required for applicant city to recommend specific sampling points and to discuss with UNESCO and technical advisor before making a final decision.

5.5.3 Sampling

Sampling will be performed by water supply operators in applicant city and supervised by UNESCO, technical advisor and/or national surveillance agency randomly in a part.

5.5.4 Quantitative assessment parameters

Quantitative assessment (drinking water quality) parameters include national standard parameters of applicant city (Group I) and required water supply parameters (Group II). [Annex 5]

| Group | Title | Explain | Weight |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ⅰ | National standard parameters [Annex 5.1] | Established by national standards of applicant city. Assess all water treatment plants (treated water) in applicant city | 50% |
| Ⅱ | Required water supply parameters [Annex5.2] | Essential parameters for drinking water safety in supply area and tap. They might be modified by water supply environment, water contamination, pipeline materials, etc. Assess all sampling locations in | 50% |

applicant city.

(1) National standard parameters

All water treatment plants in applicant city must be assessed according to the national drinking water quality standard parameters established by Government.

(2) Required water supply parameters

All sampling locations at water supply area and tap water must be assessed according to the 9 parameters in order to ensure drinking water safety and sanitation.

5.5.5 Analysis frequency

Analysis shall be performed quarterly considering seasonal variability.

5.5.6 Analysis organization

Analysis on the water quality shall be conducted by ISO certified laboratory in the country where applicant city belongs to. If it is not possible for internationally certified analysis organization to analyze under local circumstances, it is possible to be conducted by laboratory designated by the country. Any laboratory under applicant city organization cannot perform analysis for the assessment.

5.5.7 Calculation of analysis

(1) The quantitative assessment score is 100% that means treated and supplied water quality standards compliance rate.

.

## $\frac{ofWTPcompliance}{ofGroupIparameters\times ofWTPsamples\times 4\times}\times 50$

## $+\frac{ofwater^{plycompl}iance}{ofGroupIIparameters\times ofwater^{plysampl}es\times 4\times}\times 50$

Ex.) ① Number of samples: 5 for WTP, 95 for water supply area (Population: 1,500,000)

② Water quality parameters: 60 for WTP (Group I), 8 for water supply area (Group II)

③ Number of water quality standard compliance: 1200 for WTP, 2500 for water supply area ⇨ The quantitative assessment score:

5.5.8 Submission of the assessment report

(1) Quarterly report

Technical advisor submits water quality analysis report quarterly to UNESCO. [Annex 4]. The report shall be submitted with test report and raw data provided by ISO certified laboratory.

(2) Final report

Technical advisor will prepare final water quality analysis report for the seal which will be submitted to UNESCO. Technical advisor shall notify final report to applicant city before submission. If the final report shows that the water quality analysis exceeds the water quality standards set, then the city is required to prepare documents analyzing the problems occurred. [Annex 6]

5.5.9 Coordination of opinion from applicant city

(1) If applicant city has an opinion including problem and complaint regarding the result, it can be submitted with final report. [Annex 8]

(2) The opinion is considered by the Board of Seal of Excellence, and it can be affected to assessment result.

6. Assessment of Water Supply System Operation and Management (Qualitative Assessment)

6.1 Overview

6.1.1 Coverage

This guideline is for the assessment of water supply system operation and management in applicant city.

6.1.2 Frequency of assessment

Assessment of water supply system operation and management is performed at once.

6.2 Documents and Records Management

6.2.1 Finally confirmed assessment results of water supply system are submitted to UNESCO board of Seal of Excellence.

6.2.2 Assessment report of water supply system shall be preserved for at least three years with at least one copy by UNESCO, applicant city, and technical advisor.

6.3 Preparation of Assessment

6.3.1 Applicant city shall prepare the documents about water supply system including water resources, water treatment plants, water distribution and pipelines, and additional facilities. Applicant city submit the documents to Assessment Team and Technical advisor. [Annex3]

6.3.2 Assessment Team and Technical advisor shall review the documents and request additional information and data if needed.

6.4 Organization of Assessment Team

6.4.1 Assessment team shall be organized by experts in each field (water treatment process and water quality, distribution system, and facility management) from IHP Water Family and would have external experts such as IWA, WHO, and technical advisor if necessary.

6.4.2 Assessment team shall be four members

| Classification | Team leader | Water treatment process & water quality | Distribution system | Facility management |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Member | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |

6.4.3 Assessment team member shall implement their task under control of team leader and team leader could hold an additional position with members, if necessary. For qualification, an assessment team leader is required to have been engaged for at least 20 years in activities related to water supply system.

6.4.4 Assessment team members shall be trained by technical advisor before assessment of water supply system operation and management.

6.5 Assessment Scope

6.5.1 Qualitative assessment scope basically shall include water source, water treatment plant, and distribution system of applicant city. All water sources and water treatment plants in operation shall be assessed by considering their water volume weight in total water supply.

6.5.2 Identify obstacles and hazards on sustainable water cycle and try to seek the solution and alternatives.

6.5.3 Suspended facilities of water supply system shall be excluded from the assessment targets, but the causes of suspension shall be clearly indicated. If assessment team and technical advisor decide suspended facilities are needed for securing the safety of drinking water, the facilities shall be included in assessment targets.

6.6 Assessment Procedures

6.6.1 Overview

| Preliminary meeting (1st day) | Share and understand the information of water supply system operation and management of applicant city. |
| --- | --- |
| Review documents (2\~3rd day) | Review documents for the qualitative assessment submitted by applicant city. |
| Performance assessment with field survey (4\~9th day) | Perform field survey and interview with operators. Assess performance indicators according to the assessment tool-kit. |
| Assessment result meeting (10th day) | Discuss and confirm the final assessment results. |

6.6.2 Preparations of applicant city for assessment

(1) Prepare the following information of water supply system.

- National or Relevant water quality standards.
- Describing treated and supplied water quality for the last 3 years.
- Describing flow diagram from water source to distribution network.
- Describing detailed information of water source
- Describing detailed water supply system including facility, equipment, treatment process, laboratory, operation and management, etc.
- Describing detailed water treatment process including coagulation, flocculation, sedimentation, filtration, adsorption, disinfection, clearwell storage and all chemical addition points in each plant.
- Describing detailed information of water source characteristics

(2) Prepare the following including room and evidence materials for assessment.

- Discussion room including the laptop
- Designate the hands-on working group in applicant city who can interview in English for field survey in each assessment.

6.6.3 Preliminary meeting

(1) Assessment team shall share the direction of assessment, contents, and assignment of team members.

(2) Applicant city shall explain the detailed information of water supply system operation and management to Assessment team.

6.6.4 Review document and performance assessment with field survey

(1) Assessment team shall review documents for the qualitative assessment submitted by applicant city.

(2) Assessment team check according to assessment handbook in connection with field survey (water quality management with turbidity and residual chlorine, quantity management with flow and pressure) and technical advisor advise to assessment team about specific issues for assessment, if needed. [Annex10]

6.6.5 Assessment result meeting

(1) Assessment team and technical advisor shall discuss the following on the assessment results and prepare for the result report according to the form. [Annex 4.1, 4.2,4.3]

- Assessment team shall determine the results of assessment derived from field survey.
- Based on the results of the meeting, the leader of assessment team reports on the final results with good points and weak points briefly of system (not including specific scores) and listens the opinions of the applicant city.

6.6.6 Submit of the final assessment report

Assessment team and technical advisor shall discuss and verify the following on the assessment results

(1) Assessment team shall prepare the final assessment report and review it with technical advisor. [Annex 7]

(2) Assessment team and technical advisor verify final reports and submit to UNESCO.

6.6.7 Coordination of opinion from applicant city

(1) If applicant city has an opinion including problem and complaint regarding the result, it can be submitted with final report. [Annex 8]

(2) The opinion is considered by the Board of Seal of Excellence, and it can be affected to assessment result.

6.7 Qualitative Assessment Indicators

6.7.1 Indicators

Qualitative assessment is comprised of total 26 important indicators: 11 indicators for comprehensive operation, 4 indicators for water source, 7 indicators for water treatment plant, and 4 indicators for distribution system.

| Classification | Comprehensive operation | Source water | Water treatment | Distribution system | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Number of indicators | 11 | 4 | 7 | 4 | 26 |
| Total possible score | 275 | 100 | 175 | 100 | 650 |

6.7.2 Modification of indicators

Assessment indicators can be modified including addition or deletion upon the following cases.

(1) If there is need for new indicators for safe and continuous drinking water supply to cope with the local climate changes and water quality issues, then it could be modified as additional indicators.

(2) If indicators are judged to be less or not efficient in comparison with current status and international drinking water quality management, then it could be modified as indicators deleted.

6.7.3 Calculation of indicators

(1) The qualitative assessment score is 100% that means a performance rate of water supply system operation and management in applicant city.

(2) All Sources water and water treatment assessment shall be assessed by considering their water volume weight in total water supply.

Ex.) ① Total water supply: 1,000,000m3/day

- ② Number of WTP: 4, (A 200,000 m3/day, B 300,000 m3/day, C 400,000 m3/day, D 100,000 m3/day)
- ③ Calculation of indicator (Excellent score is 25)

Indicator score:

$AWTP15\times 20+BWTP25\times 30+CWTP20\times 40+DWTP25\times 10=21$

(3) The qualitative assessment score is 100% that means a performance rate of water supply system operation and management in applicant city.

![image](image_041.png)

### 7. Reference

For more information beyond this guideline, follow WHO guideline and material as below.

(1) Guideline for drinking water quality (WHO)

(1) Water safety plan manual (WHO)

(2) A practical guide to auditing water safety plans (WHO)

(3) Developing drinking water quality regulations and standards (WHO)

### Annex 1. List of Definition of Terms

1.1. Water Safety Plan (WSP)

A Water Safety Plan is a plan, which ensures the safety of drinking water with a comprehensive risk assessment, and risk management approach that encompasses all steps in water supply from catchment to consumer.

1.2. Assessment of Drinking Water Quality (Quantitative Assessment)

This is the analysis procedure through which the WHO guideline parameter will be measured at the water treatment plant and at the tap water as part of the quantitative assessment of drinking water.

1.1 Assessment of Water Supply System Operation and Management (Qualitative Assessment)

Assessment procedure by the assessment team, which includes a site visit at the applicant city and an assessment on the use of the water resources, the operation of the water treatment plant and the water supply network.

1.2 Assessment Parameters and Indicators

Direct and indirect physical, chemical and biological changes in water quality, deterioration of facilities, or operation conditions that can threaten quality of drinking water.

1.3 Assessment Score

The Assessment Score is classified into three stages namely: excellent, fair and poor. This score will be multiplied by the importance degree (A Score) and the appropriateness of operation (B Score)

1.4 Importance Degree (A Score)

Value for evaluating the water management based on the frequency of occurrence, degree of seriousness and influence on drinking water management

1.5 Appropriateness of Operation (B Score)

Value for assessing water management based on appropriateness of operation and management.

### Annex 5.

### <u>The Assessment of drinking water quality parameters</u>

1. Information for drinking water quality parameters

1.1 Quantitative assessment (drinking water quality) parameters include national standard parameters of applicant city (Group I) and required water supply parameters (Group II).

| Group | Title | Explain | Weight |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ⅰ | National standard parameters | Established by national standards of applicant city. Assess all water treatment plants (treated water) in applicant city | 50% |
| Ⅱ | Required water supply parameters | Essential parameters for drinking water safety in supply area and tap. They might be modified by water supply environment, water contamination, pipeline materials, etc. Assess all sampling locations in applicant city. | 50% |

1.2 All water treatment plants in applicant city must be assessed according to the national drinking water quality standard parameters established by Government.

1.3 Required water supply parameters shall be applied to all sampling locations at water supply area and tap water that must be assessed according to the 9 parameters in order to ensure drinking water safety and sanitation. Water Quality criteria follow the WHO guideline for drinking water quality.

1.4 Water analysis fee follow the ISO certified laboratory and national analysis fee.

1.5 Drinking water quality report shall be submitted quarterly.

1.6 Water quality analysis result by ISO certified laboratory shall be submitted with test report and raw data.

2. Detail rules for required water supply parameters

2.1 The applicant city may request UNESCO to adjust some analysis parameters considering water environment and water treatment process.

to UNESCO Director-General Annex 10. Tool for Assessment of Water Supply System Operation and Management

UNESCO Seal of Excellence for Urban Water Management

# Tool for Assessment of Water Supply System Operation and Management

2022

1. Introduction

1.1 The purpose of water supply system operation and management assessment is to ensure the safety of tap water quality and stability of tap water supply by managing all possible hazards in water supply system.

1.2 The purpose of this handbook is to provide criteria of performance for 26 water supply system operation and management.

1.3 Classifications are comprised of 4 classifications of I) comprehensive operation, II) source water, III) water treatment, and IV) distribution system.

| Classification | Comprehensive operation | Source water | Water treatment | Distribution system | Total |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Number of indicators | 11 | 4 | 7 | 4 | 26 |
| Total possible score | 275 | 100 | 175 | 100 | 650 |

2. Performance elements and derivation of grade

2.1 Each of the performance element is to assess appropriateness according to criteria.

2.2 Performance contents are assessed by the Importance degree (A score) and appropriateness (B score).

2.3 Importance degree (A) and performance are classified into 5 steps according to Likert-type scale\*.

\* Likert-type scale: As a scale measuring the thought or cognition on a specific object, Likert-type scale is the most frequently used with 5scale among 3, 5 and 7scale.

2.4 Importance degree (A) is classified based on the importance of each performance for water supply system operation and management.

| Score | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Importance | Very Low | Low | Fair | High | Very High |

2.5 Appropriateness (B) is classified with 5 score as follows.

• Excellent (5): All performance criteria are satisfied with high management level

• Good (4): All performance criteria are satisfied

• Fair (3): Some of the performance criteria are not satisfied

• Poor (2): Some of the performance criteria are not satisfied and much improvement effort is needed

• Bad (1): None of the performance criteria are satisfied

2.6 Appropriateness scores are calculated in each performance by multiplying importance degree and performance.

![image](image_042.png)

| Score | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Appropriateness | Bad | Poor | Fair | Good | Excellent |

2.7 Each of the performance is assessed according to the detailed performance handbook.

2.8 If needed, it is feasible to supplement and modify performance contents in a more specific manner than in the handbook.

### This document is dated 11th October 2022

# Seal of Excellence for Urban Water Management

# Guidelines for assessment procedure

## Contents

Management 	 38

1. Purpose

The project seeks to support Member States in achieving their SDG 6.1 and SDG 14 target on universal and equitable access to safe and affordable drinking water for all. The project will not only promote the provision of safe tap water and increase the tap water consumption, but will also advance responsible water resources consumption, increase public awareness and support enhancement of the management of the water system. The project, by reducing the number of plastic bottles used and discarded per year, will contribute to the mitigation of climate change and minimization of waste being landfilled or finding its way to the water bodies and aquatic life.

Each Sealed City will have to identify another city in a developing country to twin and support technically, in order for the latter to improve its capacity and pursue the award process. This action will promote international cooperation and technology transfer, contributing to Member States efforts in achieving SDG target 17.6.

The purpose of this guideline is to present the assessment procedure for the UNESCO Seal of Excellence for Urban Water Management. The procedure is for the assessment of (i) the operation and <u>management; and (ii)</u> drinking-water quality of the urban water supply system in the Applicant City ~~in a developed country~~.

2. Scope of Project

2.1 This guideline is prepared to be consistent with the Guidelines for Drinking-water Quality by the World Health Organization (WHO 2022) and Water Safety Plan (WSP) resources by WHO and the International Water Association (IWA).

2.2 The Seal of Excellence assessment is framed around effective implementation of water safety plans (WSPs). This is because the most effective means of consistently ensuring the safety of a drinking-water supply is through the use of a comprehensive risk assessment and risk management approach that encompasses all steps in the water supply from catchment to consumer (i.e. water safety plans or similar). This approach entails systematic assessment of risks throughout a drinking-water supply—from the catchment and its source water through to the consumer—and identification of the ways in which these risks can be managed, including methods to ensure that control measures are working effectively. Water Safety Planning incorporates strategies to deal with day-to-day management of water quality, including up-sets and failures,20 because evidence of safe drinking-water supply under normal conditions is not sufficient to demonstrate safety; control measures must also be able to be effective under exceptional conditions.

3. Roles and Responsibilities

4. Procedures for the Seal

4.1 Seal Criteria

An Applicant City is “sealed” by assessments described in this document. These are further described in chapter 5 (Drinking-water Quality Assessment), and chapter 6 (Operation and Management Assessment).

4.2 Seal Process

## 4.2.1 <u>Step 1. Applying</u> for the <u>Seal</u>

(1) Applicant City prepares the application form [Annex 2]and submits it to UNESCO.

(2) UNESCO reviews the application form and decides whether to proceed or not to proceed with the application.

## 4.2.2 <u>Step 2. Organizing the Seal assessment units</u>

(1) UNESCO requests additional documents of related to the water supply system and the application fee. [Annex 3]

(2) Applicant City prepares and provides the additional information and documents and submits it to UNESCO with the application fee.

21 Or an approved third party certification system (see abbreviations)

(3) UNESCO organizes the Seal assessment units in order to proceed with the assessment.

## 4.2.3 <u>Step 3. Establishing the details of Seal assessment</u>

(1) For the Operation and Management Assessment, UNESCO requests both the assessment team and the Technical Advisor to review the documentation and relevant information in advance. UNESCO also builds the detailed field inspection/site visit schedule in consultation with assessment team and applicant cit

(2) For Drinking-water Quality Assessment, UNESCO requests both the Technical Advisor and the Applicant City to agree on water quality parameters and choose an ISO (or equivalent third part certification system) certified laboratory for water quality analysis. UNESCO then confirms water quality parameters for testing and who, when, where, and how to take samples with the Technical Advisor, applicant city, and the ISO certified laboratory.

## 4.2.4 <u>Step 4. Doing the assessment</u>

4.2.4.1 Operation and Management Assessment

(1) Prior to the field inspection/site visit, the Assessment Team shall understand the Seal assessment tool-kit usage. [Annex 10]

(2) The Assessment Team assesses the performance of water supply system operation and management [Annex 4-1, 4-2, 4-3] using tool-kit (Annex 10) with the Technical Advisor providing, if requested by the Assessment Team, any required guidance on interpreting the Annexes and the tool kit.

(3) The Operation and Management Assessment shall be conducted once during a year.

(3) When the Operation and Management Assessment is finished, the Assessment Team submits the results to Technical Advisor.

4.2.4.2 Drinking-water Quality Assessment

(2) UNESCO (or Technical Advisor) request the approved ISO certified laboratory to begin water quality analysis. An ISO certified laboratory shall report the results to Technical Advisor immediately after finishing water quality analysis. [Annex 4].

(2) The Technical Advisor, and/or national or sub-national surveillance agency supervise water sampling if necessary.

(3) Drinking-water Quality Assessment shall be conducted quarterly during a year.

## 4.2.5 <u>Step 5. Verifying and reporting the assessment results</u>

(1) ISO certified laboratory and Technical Advisor verify the Drinking-water Quality Assessment results. The Technical Advisor submits the final report of Drinking-water Quality Assessment and the results to UNESCO. [Annex 6]

(2) The Assessment Team and Technical Advisor verify the Operation and Management Assessment results. The Technical Advisor submits the final report of Operation and Management Assessment and its results to UNESCO. [Annex 7]

(3) UNESCO reviews the final assessment reports and submits them to The Board of Seal of Excellence.

(4) The Board of Seal of Excellence reviews the final assessment reports and makes the final decision. If Applicant City doesn’t agree with the final decision, it can ask for coordination/clarification. [Annex 8]

## 4.2.6 <u>Step 6. Awarding the Seal of Excellence</u>

(1) UNESCO awards the Seal of Excellence to the applicant city, depending on the final decision of Board of Seal of Excellence.

(1) The Sealed City pays the membership fee annually and joins a knowledge-sharing platform which is coordinated by UNESCO.

(2) UNESCO informs the successful achievement of Sealed City to UNESCO members. No information related to the application will be shared nor any unsuccessful assessment.

4.3 Award system

The category of Seal of Excellence Award is dependent on the performance of both the Operation and Management Assessment, and the Drinking-water Quality Assessment results as shown in Table 1:

To be awarded a Seal of Excellence, a 97% score must be achieved for the Drinking-water Quality Assessment and the Operation and Management score must be more than 50%.

<u>DJ comment</u>: This scoring system would benefit from further expert and practitioner input. Note the option noted in paragraph 5.5.8 regarding the use of thresholds for critical parameters like E. Coli. For example. An important consideration: Is it reasonable fir UNESCO to give Star ratings in systems where there is not 100% compliance in the samples for E. Coli?

The percents noted in Table 1 are also open to debate.

The Applicant City for this Seal shall pay the application fee and assessment fee as shown in Table 2. The Assessment fee is the actual costs that include airfare, staying costs, labour fee, and training expense for Assessment Team members. The Sealed city shall pay the membership fee annually as follows.

4.5 Validity period of Seal of Excellence

Validity period is four years. After the first seal, audit (re-assessment) for the Seal validity is performed at three yearly intervals as shown in the guideline.

5. Drinking Water Quality Assessment

5.1 Overview

5.1.1 Coverage

This guideline is for the assessment of drinking water quality in the applicant city.

5.1.2 Frequency of assessment

Assessment of drinking water is performed quarterly (4 times per year)22.

5.2 Documents and Records Management

5.2.1 Final confirmed assessment report of drinking water is submitted to the Board of Seal of Excellence.

5.2.2 Assessment Report of drinking water quality shall be preserved for at least three years with at least one copy by UNESCO, applicant city, and Technical Advisor.

5.3 Preparation of Assessment

5.3.1 UNESCO shall send the document form of drinking water analysis information to the Applicant City just after approval of UNESCO. The Applicant City creates the document and submits to UNESCO. (Annex 3)???

5.3.2 If all documents are acceptable, UNESCO will select an ISO certified laboratory. The Applicant City shall request testing of the drinking water quality from the UNESCO designated ISO certified laboratory.

5.3.3 UNESCO selects sampling and observation protocols.

5.4 Assessment Scope

5.4.1 The scope of the Drinking-water Quality Assessment is a water supply system from the water treatment plant (treated water) to the water supply area (distribution network) and consumer taps in the applicant city.

5.5 Assessment Procedures

5.5.1 Determination of the number of samples for analysis:

Sampling for analysis shall be the water treatment plants, water supply area (distribution network) and consumer taps in applicant city.

(1) Water treatment plants – samples shall be taken at each operational plant.

(2) The number of sample sites in water supply area and consumer taps for non-microbial indicators shall be determined by the water supply population of Applicant City as per Table 3.

<table>
<tr><th><u>DJ comment</u>: The number of sample sites shown in this table is as per UNESCO’s latest draft (31st August 2022). This would benefit from further expert and practitioner review. One issue to consider is that the parameters assessed in the distribution network are almost all microbial and health based parameters (e.g. E coli, Turbidity, pH, DBPs (Groups II and III) with the occasional aesthetic related parameter and some priority chemicals in Group IV). So, the WHO Guidelines for sample numbers for faecal indicator testing in distribution systems noted in WHO’s Guidelines for drinking-water Quality, 4th Edition, Table 4.4 could arguably be applicable. (as these frequencies are not going to be used for heavy metals, pesticides etc). The EU guidelines have a different guide. By way of comparison, for a population of 500,000:<br>· WHO Guidelines suggest the number of sample sites per quarter would be: 3 per 50 000 population plus an additional 150 samples – i.e. 180<br>· <u>EU Directive (Annex II Part B)</u> indicates 90 samples</th></tr>

<tr><th colspan="2">Table 3: Sample numbers</th></tr>
<tr><td>Population</td><td>The number of samples per quarter</td></tr>
<tr><td>&lt;5,000</td><td>10</td></tr>
<tr><td>5,000 - 100,000</td><td>25</td></tr>

22 Where national or sub-national water quality standards have more frequent sampling and testing required, the more stringent testing frequency is to be followed for Group I parameters (see paragraph 5.5.5).

5.5.2 Selection of sampling locations

Sampling points for analysis including the water treatment plants, water supply area and tap:

The Applicant City is to recommend specific sampling points and to discuss with UNESCO and Technical Advisor before a final decision is made.

Sampling sites shall meet these general principles23:

· Sampling for treatment plants shall be at the exit of the treatment plant where the water enters the distribution system.

· The monitoring sampling points in the distribution network should reflect the quality of the drinking-water that consumers receive.

· The sampling of microbial parameters or indicators should be representative in terms of exposure (e.g. more samples need to be collected in areas of higher population densities) and be designed to detect contamination if it occurs. This means that sampling locations should account for: locations within the system with long travel times; times of increased likelihood of contamination (e.g. seasonal variations or in intermittent water supplies); and deterioration in water quality as a result of the condition of the distribution system.

· Sampling locations depend on where in the system the contaminant is expected to be introduced and the likelihood that the parameter’s concentration or character will change through the water supply system:

o Parameters for which the concentration is unlikely to change after treatment (e.g. Arsenic; Nitrate (except where chloramines are being used as a residual disinfectant); Fluoride; Aluminium; Ammonia (except where chloramines are being used as a residual disinfectant); Barium; Selenium; and Radionuclides), testing at the exit of treatment plants would usually suffice.

o Parameters for which the concentration can change after treatment and through the distribution system (e.g. Microbial indicators (e.g. E.coli); THMs; Other DBPs; Chlorine residual; Ammonia (where chloramines are being used as a residual disinfectant); Taste and odours; Turbidity; Manganese; Nitrite and nitrate (where chloramines are being used as a residual disinfectant), testing within the distribution network or at consumers’ taps would usually suffice.

o Parameters for which the concentration is largely influenced by service connections or plumbing within buildings (e.g. Lead and Copper) testing should usually be at consumer’s taps24.

5.5.3 Sampling

Sampling will be performed by the water supply operators in the Applicant City and supervised by UNESCO, the Technical Advisor and/or national or sub-national surveillance agency.

It is recognized that some parameters from Groups II and III (see Table 4) may be already sampled and tested by the water supplier/Applicant City for operational reasons. Where this is the case, provided there is acceptable proof of independence in the process, the Applicant City may request that this process be accepted as part of the sampling and testing protocols.

5.5.4 Co-ordination with surveillance agency

The Technical Advisor should discuss the sampling program, including choice of parameters, with the regulatory drinking-water quality surveillance agency. Agreement reached on which parameters represent the greatest risk in the system and which microbial indicator organisms should be used in the program.

5.5.5 Drinking-water Quality Assessment parameters

Drinking-water Quality Assessment parameters are shown in Table 4.

Note 1: Exemption can be sought for parameters included in the natation or sub-national drinking-water quality standards on the basis of a system specific risk assessment. The risk assessment should include:

- · historical water quality monitoring results including evidence if the parameter is not present, or is present in concentrations well below the maximum concentrations

recommended by WHO or the drinking-water quality standards.;

- · the different sources of water (e.g. groundwater, surface water, springs);
- · potential contamination sources (e.g. industrial, agricultural); and
- · treatment processes used (e.g. chlorination or chloramination).

Note 2: If an online meter is being used to measure turbidity, frequency should be changed to daily

Note 3: If turbidity and chlorine are being measured online, the recorded daily result is the maximum result recorded in any 24-hour period

(1) National or sub-national standard parameters (Group I)

Water treatment plants in an Applicant City must be assessed according to the national or sub-national drinking water quality standard parameters established by the national or sub-national government of the Applicant City or other relevant authority.

(2) Required water supply distribution network parameters (Groups II, III and IV)

All sampling locations at water supply area and tap water must be assessed according to the required parameters. These parameters can be modified by agreement based on the water supply risk management assessments, source water quality, water contamination, pipeline materials, etc

5.5.6 Analysis frequency

The frequency of sampling is indicated in Table 4. Where there is quarterly analysis, consideration should be given to seasonal variability of raw water sources.

5.5.7 Analysis organization

Laboratory analysis on the water quality shall be conducted by ISO25 certified laboratory in the country of the applicant city, however for testing which is most appropriately done by field kits (e.g. pH, free chlorine residual) approval can be granted for approved field kit testing. If it is not possible for internationally certified analysis organization to analyze under local circumstances, it is possible to be conducted by laboratory designated by the country. The laboratory must be independent of the applicant city’s organization.

(1) The Drinking-water Quality Assessment score is calculated as follows:

<u>DJ comment</u>: It’s worth noting for consideration that a simple percent score is a somewhat simplistic approach as it does not take into account the health significance of a parameter. For example, the microbial indicator (E. Coli) in Groups II and III is much more important than some of the other parameters and it could be argued that it be given more weight.

One could, for example, introduce a threshold compliance for, say E Coli of say 100% to get any score for Group II or Group II.

5.5.9 Submission of the assessment report

(1) Quarterly report

The Technical Advisor submits water quality analysis report quarterly to UNESCO. [Annex 4]. The report shall be submitted with test report and raw data provided by the approved certified laboratory.

(2) Final report

The Technical Advisor will prepare the final water quality analysis report for the seal which will be submitted to UNESCO. The Technical Advisor shall notify the Applicant City of the final report before submission. If the final report shows that the water quality analysis exceeds the water quality standards set, then the city is required to prepare documents analyzing the problems occurred. [Annex 6]

5.5.10 Coordination of opinion from applicant city

(1) If Applicant City has an opinion including disagreement or complaint regarding the result, it can submit its opinion with the final report [Annex 8].

(2) The opinion is to be considered by the Board of Seal of Excellence, and making its decision.

6. Assessment of Water Supply System Operation and Management

6.1 Overview

6.1.1 Coverage

This guideline is for the assessment of water supply system operation and management in the Applicant City.

6.1.2 Frequency of assessment

Assessment of water supply system operation and management is performed once.

6.2 Documents and Records Management

6.2.1 The confirmed assessment results of water supply system are to be submitted to UNESCO board of Seal of Excellence.

6.2.2 The Assessment Report of water supply system shall be preserved for at least three years with at least one copy by UNESCO, applicant city, and the Technical Advisor.

6.3 Preparation of Assessment

6.3.1 The Applicant City shall prepare the documents about the water supply system’s operation and management and risk management as detailed in Annex 3. The Applicant City shall submit the documents to Assessment Team and Technical Advisor.

6.3.2 The Assessment Team and Technical Advisor shall review the documents and request additional information and data if needed.

6.4 Organization of Assessment Team

6.4.1 The Assessment Team shall be organized by experts in each field (risk management and operation of water treatment processes, water quality, distribution system, and facility management) from IHP Water Family and if necessary, have external experts such as from IWA, WHO, and the Technical Advisor.

6.4.2 The Assessment Team shall be four members

| Classification | Team leader | Water treatment process & water quality | Distribution system | Management |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Member | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |

6.4.3 Assessment Team members shall implement their task under control of the Assessment Team leader. The Assessment Team Leader may hold an additional position with members, if necessary.

6.4.4 The Assessment Team Leader is required to have been engaged for at least 20 years in activities related to drinking-water supply systems and have extensive risk management experience consistent with WHO’s Water Safety Plan or similar risk management plans.

6.4.5 Assessment Team members shall be trained by the Technical Advisor before assessment of water supply system operation and management and must be competent and experienced in water safety planning.

6.5 Assessment Scope

6.5.1 The scope of the Operation and Management Assessment shall include26:

a. appropriate organization and currency of Water Safety Planning (WSP Modules, 1, 10/11);

b. appropriate system description and identification and currency of hazards and hazardous events of the water supply system (WSP Modules 2 and 327);

c. appropriate identification and currency of existing control measures and risk assessment including critical control points, control measure verification, risk assessment methodology and application (WSP Module 4);

d. appropriate identification and implementation of improvement plans (WSP Module 5);

e. appropriate planning and implementation of operational monitoring and maintenance procedures (including control measure operational monitoring, water sampling for operational monitoring purposes, verification programs (WSP Modules 6 and 7);

f. appropriate planning and implementation of the processes for managing normal operations (including Standard Operating Procedures), incidents and emergencies including reporting requirements to the regulator and public communication (WSP Module 8); and

g. Identification and implementation of supporting programs and staff training programs (WSP Module 9).

6.5.2 The scope includes the water sources/catchment, water treatment plants, and distribution network system. All water sources and water treatment plants in operation or potential use as emergency water sources shall be assessed.

6.5.3 Suspended facilities of water supply system shall be excluded from the assessment targets, but the reasons of suspension shall be clearly indicated in the Application. If the Assessment Team and Technical Advisor decide suspended facilities are needed for securing the safety of drinking water, the facilities shall be included in assessment targets.

6.6 Assessment Procedures

6.6.1 An overview of a typical program of assessment for a large urban city is given in the following table. This timetable will be amended to suit the specific requirements and scope of scheme and documentation for each city.

| Preliminary meeting (1st day) | Share and understand the information of water supply system operation and management of applicant city. |
| --- | --- |
| Review documents (2\~3rd day) | Desktop review of documents for the Operation and Management Assessment submitted by applicant city. |
| Performance assessment with field survey (4\~9th day) | Perform field survey and interview with operators. Assess performance indicators according to the assessment tool-kit. |
| Assessment result meeting (10th day) | Discuss and confirm the final assessment results. |

6.6.2 Preparations of Applicant City for assessment

(1) Prepare the information as noted in Annex 3.

(2) Prepare the following logistical and human resources for the assessment.

- Discussion room including laptop and data projector
- Designated hands-on working group in the Applicant City who can interview in English for

26 Refer to Annex 1 for Water Safety Plan terminology.

27 Excluding risk assessment. Risk assessment in including in the following paragraph

field survey
6.6.3 Preliminary meeting
(1) Prior to the Preliminary Meeting, the Assessment Team leader shall develop a provisional
agenda for the preliminary meeting and the balance of the assessment.
(2) The Assessment Team shall share the direction of assessment, contents, and assignment of
team members including scope and program of the assessment.
(1) The Applicant City shall give an overview of the water supply system operation and
management of the system (including its risk management approaches) to the Assessment
Team and respond to questions from the Assessment Team.
(2) The timetable Meeting. 6.6.4 Review document	for the balance and performance	of the assessment	assessment shall be finalized at the Preliminary with field survey
(1) The Assessment Team is to review the documents for the Operation and Management
Assessment submitted by applicant city. A thorough assessment of the water safety plan
is required to be undertaken by the Assessment Team, including the WSP contents, an
evaluation of identified risks to the water supply, a review of the implementation of the
WSP by the water supplier and the presence and effectiveness of procedures, processes and
protocols in place to manage risks.
(2) The Assessment Team is to use the “Tool for Assessment of Water Supply Operation and
Management” [Annex10]. (3) The Technical Advisor	is to	advise Assessment	Team of specific issues for assessment in
relation to the field survey (water quality management e.g. turbidity, residual chlorine,
quantity management and flow and pressure).
(4) The Applicant city shall make available, at the request of the Assessment Team documents
as needed for the assessment like: the WSP; it's operational monitoring reports; emergency
management plans; associated policy documents; water supply plans or schematics; standard
operating procedures and protocols; incident registers; maintenance and works records;
training records; supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) systems; any documents
referred to in the WSP.
6.6.5 Assessment result meeting
(1) Prior to the assessment result meeting (on Day 10), the Assessment Team and Technical
Advisor shall discuss its findings of the desktop and field surveys and prepare for the result
report according to the forms using Annexes 4.1, 4.2 and4.3.
(2) The findings shall be presented to the Assessment result meeting.
(3) The Assessment Team Leader and, as required, other members of the Assessment Team
report on the findings, including overall findings, strengths, areas for improvement, (not
including specific scores) and invites and listens the opinions of the Applicant City in
response to these findings. The Applicant City can clarify any issues it considers are
misunderstandings in this meeting.
6.6.6 Submission of the final assessment report
(1) The Assessment Team and the Technical Advisor shall discuss and verify the final findings
following the assessment result meeting.
(2) The Assessment Team shall prepare the final assessment report for review by the Technical
Advisor. [Annex 7]
(3) The Assessment Team and the Technical Advisor verifies the final reports and submits these
to UNESCO.
6.6.7 Coordination of opinion from applicant city
(1) If Applicant City wishes to clarify the findings, including concerns or complaints regarding
the result, it can submit these with the Final report. [Annex 8]
(2) The Applicant City’s opinions are to be considered by the Board of Seal of Excellence, in
making its final assessment.
6.7 Operation and Management Assessment performance criteria and scoring
(1) The performance criteria and scoring for the Operation and Management Assessment
are detailed in Annex 10 (Tool for Water Supply Operation and Management
Assessment). Minor changes to the assessment performance criteria can be modified by

mutual agreement with the Technical Advisor, Assessment Team leader and Applicant City including addition or deletion where the performance criteria are agreed to be unimportant or not relevant in comparison with international best practice in drinking water quality risk management.

(2) Any adjustments made must maintain a consistent balance of weightings in the overall score.

(3) As noted in Annex 10 (Tool for Water Supply Operation and Management Assessment), the total score for the Operation and Management Assessment is expressed as a percent.

### 7. References

For more information beyond this guideline, follow WHO guideline and material as below.

(1) WHO (2022) Guidelines for drinking water quality (current edition28) (WHO) Guidelines for drinking-water quality, 4th edition, incorporating the 1st addendum Geneva: WHO. Available at: <u>https://www.who.int/publications/i/item/9789241549950</u> (accessed 10 September 2022).

(2) WHO (2009) Water safety plan manual (current edition)29 (WSP Manual) (WHO) available at <u>https://www.who.int/publications/i/item/9789241562638</u> accessed 10 September 2022

(3) WHO (2016) A practical guide to auditing water safety plans (WHO and IWA) Available at <u>https://apps.who.int/iris/handle/10665/204280</u> Accessed 10 September 2022

(4) WHO (2018). Developing drinking-water quality regulations and standards: general guidance with a special focus on countries with limited resources. Geneva: WHO. Available at: <u>https://apps.who.int/iris/handle/10665/272969</u> (accessed 10 September 2022).

28 At the time of preparation of this guide, the current edition was Guidelines for drinking-water quality, 4th edition, incorporating the 1st addendum

29 At the time of preparation of this guide, the current edition was the 2009 edition. It is expected that there will be a new edition issued in 2022.

### Annex 1. List of Definition of Terms and Abbreviations

## Definition of Terms:

1.1. Water Safety Plan (WSP)

A Water Safety Plan is a plan, to help ensure the safety of drinking water with a comprehensive risk assessment, and risk management approach that encompasses all steps in water supply from catchment to consumer.

1.2. Drinking-water Quality Assessment

This is the analysis procedure through which the WHO guideline parameter will be measured at the water treatment plant and at the tap water as part of the drinking-water quality assessment.

1.1 Operation and Management Assessment

Assessment procedure by the assessment team, which includes a site visit at the Applicant City and an assessment on the use of the water resources, the operation of the water treatment plant and the water supply network.

1.2 Assessment Parameters and Indicators

Direct and indirect physical, chemical, radiological and biological in water quality, deterioration of facilities, or operation conditions that can threaten quality and safety of drinking water.

1.3 Assessment Score

The Assessment Score is classified into three stages namely: excellent, fair and poor. This score will be multiplied by the importance degree (A Score) and the appropriateness of operation (B Score)

1.4 Importance Degree (A Score)

Value for evaluating the water management based on the frequency of occurrence, degree of seriousness and influence on drinking water management

1.5 Appropriateness of Operation (B Score)

Value for assessing water management based on appropriateness of operation and management.

## Key Water Safety Plan terminology:

1.1 Control measure:

An activity or process to prevent, eliminate or reduce the risk of a hazardous event to an acceptable level.

1.2 Control measure validation:

obtaining evidence that the control measure can effectively control the corresponding hazardous event.

1.3 Critical Control Points

A step in the process at which control can be applied and is essential to prevent, control, or eliminate a drinking-water safety hazard or reduce it to an acceptable level.

1.4 Hazard:

A contaminant or condition which may adversely affect the supply of safe drinking-water.

1.5 Hazardous event:

An event by which the hazard is introduced to, or is inadequately removed from, the water supply.

1.6 Improvement plan:

An action plan for improving the level of control for a hazardous event, thereby reducing the level of risk.

1.7 Operational monitoring

Monitoring of control measures to ensure they work as intended, and that proper and timely corrective action is taken when pre-defined limits are not met

1.8 Risk

The product of the likelihood of occurrence and the severity (or consequences) of a hazardous event

1.9 Risk assessment:

An evaluation of the significance of the hazardous event.

Standard operating procedure:

A set of step-by-step instructions to guide staff when carrying out routine tasks either under normal or incident conditions.

<tr><td></td><td>1.11 WSP Team:</td><td></td><td>The team that leads the development and ongoing implementation of the WSP.</td></tr>

### Annex 3. Additional Water Supply System Information

### Filled in by applicant city

### <u>Additional Water Supply System Information</u>

1. General Information

| Name of city | Contact person |
| --- | --- |
|  | Name(organization): Contact: e-mail: |

Provide a copy a current version the City’s Water Safety Plan.

As a minimum this plan should include:

<u>WSP Team</u>: including organizational commitment to water safety planning, team members and meeting schedules

## <u>System description</u>:

This would typically include descriptive text, alongside summarized tables/analyses, relevant figures (e.g. trends) and the system diagram(s). The following information will assist the Assessment Team:

1. general information about the water supplier and the water supply (e.g. scale of the system, areas of responsibility)
2. the boundaries of the WSP (e.g. catchment boundaries, bulk supplier/distribution system interface(s) and user boundaries e.g. is it at the water meter or does it include user aspects beyond the water meter)
3. the intended uses of the water (e.g. potable use, including for ingestion, food preparation and other household applications)
4. the full diversity of water users (e.g. households, institutions such as schools and healthcare facilities, commercial/industrial users, informal settlements)
5. catchment characteristics including extent of vegetation coverage, topography, soil types, condition, protection areas, recharge zones (for groundwater sources), land uses and activities
6. all current sources of water for the water supply (including primary and alternative or emergency sources) and their typical yield ranges
7. raw water in-take, storage and conveyance to the water treatment plant(s)
8. the various water treatment processes (e.g. design capacity, daily production history range in past 3 years, pre-treatment, coagulation/flocculation, clarification, filtration, disinfection, including any chemicals used to treat the water) and a summary of their treatment performance
9. distribution system(s) including treated water storages, the piped distribution network (including a summary of the age, condition, size/capacity, and the materials in contact with the drinking-water, disinfection/booster chlorination facilities, pressure management)
10. user interfaces/connections (e.g. kiosks, tap stands, water carting, piped connections to premises)
11. user-level practices (e.g. where no piped connections to premises: collection/transportation, household-level treatment, storage and handling)
12. water demand (including seasonal patterns)
13. summary of water quality targets (e.g. requirements of national or sub-national drinking-water quality regulations and standards)
14. summary of historical water quality, with a particular focus on non-compliances (see tables below)
15. known or potential problems throughout the system (including a summary of recurrent consumer complaints)
16. uncertainties that may exist for specific parts of the system (e.g. lack of information on pipe location)
17. history and trends of extreme weather events
18. future trends e.g. current and projected impacts from climate variability and change, anticipated future water resource demand (e.g. arising from changes in abstraction patterns, catchment development, land use, urbanization and population growth (see Box III-2.3 for examples of climate-related data sources and information)
19. potential future or alternative (including emergency) water sources and any anticipated drinking-water safety implications from these sources.

System Description item 14: Suggested format for summary of historical water quality and compliance in last 3 years

Note: as an alternative to this format, the three most recent formal annual water quality reports can be provided if this gives similar information.

Description of Supplied Water Quality and Compliance in last 3 years

1: Operating targets are those selected by the utility provider and noted in their operating manuals or Operational Plans as target. These would usually be more strict than the regulatory targets.

2: Where the limit or target is not a maximum value (e.g it is a range or minimum), adjust as required.

## <u>Risk assessment</u>

This would typically be included in a tabular spreadsheet format and would include: risk assessment methodology/risk matrix/matrices/definitions, hazards and hazardous events, risk assessment/risk levels, existing control measures, control measure validation, risk prioritization

## <u>Improvement plans</u>

Detailed improvement plan for hazardous events that require additional control and associated implementation records.

<u>Operational monitoring</u>

- · Identification of the control measures monitored
- · Detailed operational monitoring plan for the relevant control measures, including performance limits, and corrective actions
- · Implementation records of the operational monitoring

<u>Verification</u>

Summary of methods and frequency used to verify that the WSP, as a whole, is working effectively including: compliance monitoring, user satisfaction monitoring and WSP auditing.

Associated verification implementation records

## <u>WSP management procedures:</u>

Overview of Standard operating procedures (SOPs), and Emergency response plans (ERPs) and summary of their implementation

## <u>WSP supporting programs</u>

An overview of the programmes that support WSP implementation, and a summary of their implementation.

## <u>WSP Reviews and updates</u>

An overview of the WSP review processes including: regular WSP reviews to ensure the WSP is kept up-to-date; reviews of the WSP after significant incidents, near-misses or emergencies etc and a summary of reviews conducted in the last three years.

## Annex 10

### Tool for Water Supply System Operation and Management Assessment

### Guidelines for Scoring

There are four assessment classifications. Each assessment classification is comprised of several performance criteria:

| Assessment classifications | Performance criteria | Simplified description of performance criteria topics |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Comprehensive management (CM) | CM-1 to CM-10 | WSP Modules 1, 9, 10/11 & general management issues |
| Water Source (WS) | WS-1 to WS-17 | WSP Modules 2-8 |
| Water Treatment (WT) | WT-1 to WT-18 | WSP Modules 2-8 |
| Distribution System (DS) | DS-1 to DS-19 | WSP Modules 2-8 |

Scoring is carried out for each performance criteria based on a pre-defined 1-5 scale.

There are two types of scoring guides:

5 Likert scale:

| Emerging | Progressing | Good | Very Good | Excellent |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |

following these descriptors:

| Excellent: | All performance criteria are fully satisfied with some exceeded |
| --- | --- |
| Very good: | All performance criteria are fully satisfied |
| Good: | Most of the performance criteria are fully satisfied |
| Progressing: | Most of the performance criteria are partially satisfied and progress is being made over time |
| Emerging: | Some performance criteria are partially satisfied |

The 5 Likert scale is used where there is scope to differentiate between acceptability and a distinguished performance. In this scale, 3, 4 or 5 would be acceptable.

<u>3 Likert scale</u>

The 3 Likert scale is used where performance of the criteria is straightforward and there is limited scope to distinguish between a satisfactory (or acceptable) performance and a more outstanding performance. In this scale, 3 or 5 are both acceptable performances.

| Emerging | Satisfactory | Exemplary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 3 | 5 |

flowing these descriptors:

| Exemplary | The performance is above the requirements |
| --- | --- |
| Satisfactory | The performance meets the requirements |
| Emerging: | The performance is below the requirements |

Intermediate scores can be allocated if the Assessment Team considers this necessary but are not encouraged.

The total score for the assessment of the Water Supply System Operation and Management is based on an equal weighting between each Assessment Classification.

<table>
<tr><th colspan="2">Weightings between Assessment Classifications</th></tr>
<tr><td>Assessment Classifications</td><td>Weightin g</td></tr>
<tr><td>Comprehensive management</td><td>25%</td></tr>
<tr><td>Source/catchment</td><td>25%</td></tr>
<tr><td>WTP</td><td>25%</td></tr>
<tr><td>Distribution system</td><td>25%</td></tr>
<tr><td>Total</td><td>100%</td></tr>
</table>

The performance criteria scores are also weighted to reflect the relative importance of each performance criteria for that particular assessment classification as shown in the following tables.

Explanation Note: This is not for inclusion in the final toolbox. It is for the information of reviewers only.

Modules 1,9 and 10/11 are included in the Comprehensive management classification. This graph shows the weighting of the WSP modules within the other three assessment classifications:

All weights are based on the suggested weightings in WHO/IWA (2016) “A practical guide to auditing water safety plans” Toolbox B.2.iii according to the WSP Module under consideration.

<table>
<tr><th colspan="2">Assessment classification Comprehensive management</th></tr>
<tr><td>Performance Criteria</td><td>Weight of individual performance criteria score over comprehensive management classification</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-1 Organization commitment</td><td>15.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-2 WSP Team</td><td>10.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-3 Stakeholder engagement and customer communications</td><td>15.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-4 Multiple systems (see note)</td><td>5.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-5 Other management systems (see note)</td><td>7.5%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-6: WSP reviews</td><td>10.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-7: Climate resilience</td><td>10.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-8 Equity considerations</td><td>5.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-9 Adequacy and reliability of supply</td><td>10.0%</td></tr>
<tr><td>CM-10 Management and Planning</td><td>12.5%</td></tr>
<tr><td>TOTAL</td><td>100%</td></tr>
</table>

NOTE: If this performance criteria is not relevant, weighting for all other performance criteria in the Comprehensive Management Classification is to be adjusted accordingly.

Maximum possible score: 5 (based on a score of 5 for each criterium, weighted as shown)

Interpretation of scoring of the operational and management assessment

| Total score | Comments | When combined with meeting the drinking-water quality assessment threshold this results in: |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ≥ 60% <<br>72% | The lower bound of this range corresponds to:<br>· a “good” score (3) for all performance criteria that have the 5-Likert scale, and<br>· a “satisfactory” score (3) for all performance criteria that have the 3-Likert scale | A one star award |
| - ≥ 72% <<br>85% | The lower bound of this range corresponds to:<br>· a “very good” score (4) for all performance criteria that have the 5-Likert scale (i.e. a score), and<br>· a “satisfactory” score of 3 for all performance criteria that have the 3-Likert scale | A two star award |
| - ≥ 85% | This represents exceptional performance. | A three star award |
